`playbooks/cards/cpr-adult.md` (complete):

```markdown
---
id: cpr-adult
title: CPR, adult
icon: heart
order: 1
summary: Chest compressions and rescue breaths for an adult who is not breathing normally.
---

## When to use

Someone has collapsed, does not respond when you shout and shake them, and is not breathing normally.

## Steps

1. Shout for help. Call 999 on speaker and put the phone beside you.
2. Send someone for a defibrillator (AED) if one is nearby.
3. Kneel beside them. Heel of one hand on the centre of the chest.
4. Other hand on top, arms straight. Press down 5 to 6 cm, 100 to 120 times a minute (two a second), letting the chest come back up fully each time.
5. After 30 presses, tilt the head back, lift the chin, pinch the nose and give 2 breaths, each one second, watching the chest rise. If you cannot or will not give breaths, keep pressing without stopping.
6. Carry on 30 presses then 2 breaths. Swap with someone every two minutes if you can.
7. When the AED arrives, turn it on and follow its voice instructions; keep pressing while the pads go on.
8. Do not stop until they breathe normally, help takes over, or you are exhausted.

## Warnings

**Warning:** Gasping, snoring or occasional gulps are not normal breathing. Start CPR.
**Warning:** Broken ribs are common and do not mean stop.

## Stop or escalate

999 is already on the way. Stop only when the person breathes normally on their own (then use the [recovery position](card:recovery-position)) or a paramedic takes over.

## Source

[CPR (NHS)](kiwix:nhs_uk/www.nhs.uk/conditions/first-aid/cpr/); [Ship Captain's Medical Guide ch. 1, p. 12](doc:scmg-ch01#page=12); [Cardiopulmonary resuscitation (Wikipedia)](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Cardiopulmonary_resuscitation).
```

`playbooks/cards/cpr-child.md`:

```markdown
---
id: cpr-child
title: CPR, child and baby
icon: child
order: 2
summary: Rescue breaths first, then compressions, for a child from birth to puberty who is not breathing normally.
---

## When to use

A child or baby does not respond and is not breathing normally.

## Steps

1. Shout for help. Alone? Do one minute of CPR, then call 999.
2. Tilt the head back a little, lift the chin, and give 5 rescue breaths.
3. Baby: seal your mouth over mouth and nose. Child: pinch the nose.
4. Press the centre of the chest 30 times at 100 to 120 a minute: a baby with two fingers about 4 cm deep, a child with the heel of one hand about 5 cm deep (a third of the chest).
5. Give 2 breaths, then 30 more presses. Keep going 30 to 2.
6. Call 999 on speaker after the first minute if nobody else has, and use a defibrillator if one arrives (paediatric pads or setting if available; adult pads if not).
7. Do not stop until the child breathes normally or help takes over.

## Warnings

**Warning:** Do not tilt a baby's head far back; a neutral position keeps the airway open.
**Warning:** Never shake a child to wake them.

## Stop or escalate

999 straight away if someone else can call; otherwise after one minute of CPR. Stop only when the child breathes normally (then the [recovery position](card:recovery-position)) or a paramedic takes over.

## Source

[CPR (NHS)](kiwix:nhs_uk/www.nhs.uk/conditions/first-aid/cpr/); [Ship Captain's Medical Guide ch. 1, p. 12](doc:scmg-ch01#page=12); [Cardiopulmonary resuscitation (Wikipedia)](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Cardiopulmonary_resuscitation).
```

`playbooks/cards/severe-bleeding.md`:

```markdown
---
id: severe-bleeding
title: Severe bleeding
icon: blood
order: 3
summary: Stopping heavy bleeding with pressure, packing and a tourniquet.
---

## When to use

Blood is flowing or spurting, soaking through cloth, or pooling; the person is pale, faint or confused.

## Steps

1. Call 999. Put on gloves if you have them.
2. Press hard on the wound with a pad or clean cloth. Do not let go.
3. Keep pressing for at least 10 minutes without lifting to look.
4. If blood soaks through, add more on top; never remove the first pad.
5. For a deep wound in the groin, armpit or neck, pack the wound tightly with gauze or clean cloth and press on the packing.
6. For a limb wound that will not stop, put a tourniquet 5 to 7 cm above the wound, not over a joint, tighten until the bleeding stops, and write the time on it. Do not loosen it.
7. Lie the person down, keep them warm, and treat for [shock](card:shock).
8. Do not give food or drink.

## Warnings

**Warning:** A tourniquet hurts; that is expected. Leave it on for the paramedics.
**Warning:** Do not remove an object stuck in a wound; press around it.

## Stop or escalate

999 at once. If they stop breathing, start [CPR](card:cpr-adult).

## Source

[Ship Captain's Medical Guide ch. 1, p. 15](doc:scmg-ch01#page=15); [FM 4-25.11, p. 41](doc:fm-4-25-11-first-aid#page=41); [Cuts and grazes (NHS)](kiwix:nhs_uk/www.nhs.uk/conditions/cuts-and-grazes/).
```

`playbooks/cards/choking.md`:

```markdown
---
id: choking
title: Choking
icon: lungs
order: 4
summary: Back blows and abdominal thrusts for an adult or child, chest thrusts for a baby.
---

## When to use

Someone cannot breathe, cough or speak, is clutching their throat, or is turning blue.

## Steps

1. If they can cough, tell them to keep coughing. Stay with them.
2. If not: lean them forward, 5 hard blows between the shoulder blades.
3. Check the mouth after each blow and remove anything you can see.
4. If still choking: stand behind, fist above the navel, other hand over it, pull sharply inwards and upwards 5 times.
5. Keep alternating 5 back blows and 5 abdominal thrusts.
6. Baby under one: lay face down along your forearm, head low, 5 back blows; then face up, 5 chest thrusts with two fingers on the breastbone. Never abdominal thrusts on a baby.
7. If they become unresponsive, start [CPR](card:cpr-adult) and call 999.

## Warnings

**Warning:** Anyone who has had abdominal thrusts must be checked by a doctor afterwards.
**Warning:** Do not sweep the mouth blindly with a finger.

## Stop or escalate

Call 999 if the blockage does not clear after the first cycle or the person becomes unresponsive.

## Source

[Ship Captain's Medical Guide ch. 1, p. 14](doc:scmg-ch01#page=14); [Choking (Wikipedia)](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Choking).
```

`playbooks/cards/burns.md`:

```markdown
---
id: burns
title: Burns and scalds
icon: fire
order: 5
summary: Cool, cover, and know which burns need hospital.
---

## When to use

Skin is burned by heat, steam, hot liquid, chemicals or electricity.

## Steps

1. Move away from the heat. Stop, drop and roll if clothing is alight.
2. Cool the burn under cool running water for 20 minutes.
3. Take off jewellery and loose clothing near it; leave stuck cloth.
4. Cooling helps up to three hours after the burn. Never use ice, butter, creams or toothpaste.
5. Cover loosely with cling film laid lengthways, or a clean non-fluffy cloth or plastic bag. Do not wrap tightly.
6. Give paracetamol for pain (adult 1 g, see the medicine pack). Keep the person warm; a big burn loses heat fast.
7. Chemical burns: brush off dry powder, then rinse for 20 minutes or more; keep the run-off away from you.

## Warnings

**Warning:** A burn larger than the person's hand, deep, white or charred, or on the face, hands, feet, genitals or across a joint needs hospital.
**Warning:** Do not burst blisters.

## Stop or escalate

999 for any large or deep burn, any electrical or chemical burn, burns to the face or airway, difficulty breathing, or a burned baby, child or older person.

## Source

[Burns and scalds (NHS)](kiwix:nhs_uk/www.nhs.uk/conditions/burns-and-scalds/); [Ship Captain's Medical Guide ch. 1, p. 13](doc:scmg-ch01#page=13); [Emergency War Surgery, p. 29](doc:emergency-war-surgery-2018#page=29).
```

`playbooks/cards/hypothermia.md`:

```markdown
---
id: hypothermia
title: Hypothermia
icon: snowflake
order: 6
summary: Recognising and rewarming someone whose body temperature has dropped below 35 °C.
---

## When to use

Shivering, cold pale skin, slurred speech, clumsiness, confusion; later shivering stops and they go drowsy.

## Steps

1. Get them out of the wind and wet, into shelter.
2. Take off wet clothes. Wrap them in dry layers, blankets and a hat.
3. Insulate from the ground: mat, cardboard, spare clothes underneath.
4. Give warm sweet drinks if they are awake and can swallow. No alcohol.
5. Warm the trunk first: hot-water bottles or warm packs wrapped in cloth at the chest, armpits and groin, or share body heat under blankets.
6. Handle gently and keep them lying flat; sudden movement can stop the heart.
7. Do not rub the limbs, do not use a hot bath, and do not leave them alone.

## Warnings

**Warning:** A very cold person can look dead. Check breathing for a full minute before deciding.
**Warning:** Rewarming the arms and legs first sends cold blood to the heart.

## Stop or escalate

999 if they are confused, drowsy, have stopped shivering, or are a baby or an older person. If they stop breathing, start [CPR](card:cpr-adult) and keep going; cold people have survived long resuscitation.

## Source

[Hypothermia (NHS)](kiwix:nhs_uk/www.nhs.uk/conditions/hypothermia/); [Ship Captain's Medical Guide ch. 11, p. 1](doc:scmg-ch11#page=1); [FM 4-25.11, p. 118](doc:fm-4-25-11-first-aid#page=118).
```

`playbooks/cards/heat-stroke.md`:

```markdown
---
id: heat-stroke
title: Heat stroke
icon: thermometer
order: 7
summary: Cooling someone fast when heat exhaustion has become heat stroke.
---

## When to use

Hot skin, confusion, fast breathing, fits, or no better 30 minutes after cooling; temperature over 40 °C.

## Steps

1. Call 999. Heat stroke can kill within the hour.
2. Move them to the coolest shaded place. Take off outer clothing.
3. Cool with cool water: sponge, spray or pour it over them and fan.
4. Put cold packs or wet cloths on the neck, armpits and groin.
5. If they are fully awake, give sips of water or oral rehydration solution.
6. If they are drowsy or fitting, put them in the [recovery position](card:recovery-position) and give nothing by mouth.
7. Keep cooling until they are alert and their skin feels normal, then keep them in the shade and watch them.

## Warnings

**Warning:** Heat exhaustion (sweating, dizziness, cramps, nausea) is the warning stage: cool down and rehydrate within 30 minutes.
**Warning:** Children, older people and anyone on diuretics or some heart or mental-health medicines are at most risk.

## Stop or escalate

999 for confusion, fits, loss of consciousness, or no improvement after 30 minutes of cooling.

## Source

[Heat exhaustion and heatstroke (NHS)](kiwix:nhs_uk/www.nhs.uk/conditions/heat-exhaustion-heatstroke/); [Adverse Weather and Health Plan, p. 34](doc:awhp-2026#page=34); [Beat the heat (GOV.UK)](kiwix:govuk_resilience/www.gov.uk/guidance/beat-the-heat-hot-weather-advice).
```

`playbooks/cards/broken-bones.md`:

```markdown
---
id: broken-bones
title: Broken bones
icon: bone
order: 8
summary: Supporting a suspected fracture and knowing which need an ambulance.
---

## When to use

Pain, swelling, a limb bent the wrong way, grating, or unable to move or bear weight after an injury.

## Steps

1. Keep the injured part still in the position you found it.
2. Do not try to straighten it or push bone back in.
3. Support it with padding: cushions, rolled clothes, a sling for an arm.
4. Splint a leg to the other leg or to a board with padding between, tying above and below the break, not over it.
5. Cover any open wound with a clean dressing and control bleeding with pressure around, not on, the bone.
6. Wrapped ice or a cold pack for 20 minutes eases swelling.
7. Check colour, warmth and feeling below the injury every few minutes; loosen ties if fingers or toes go white or numb.

## Warnings

**Warning:** Do not move someone with a suspected neck, back or pelvis injury unless they are in danger.
**Warning:** Do not give food or drink; they may need an anaesthetic.

## Stop or escalate

999 for an open fracture, a thigh, hip, pelvis, spine or skull injury, an obvious deformity, numbness or a white cold limb below the injury, or heavy bleeding.

## Source

[Ship Captain's Medical Guide ch. 1, p. 8](doc:scmg-ch01#page=8); [FM 4-25.11, p. 51](doc:fm-4-25-11-first-aid#page=51); [Broken arm or wrist (NHS)](kiwix:nhs_uk/www.nhs.uk/conditions/broken-arm-or-wrist/).
```

`playbooks/cards/radiation-sickness.md`:

```markdown
---
id: radiation-sickness
title: Radiation sickness
icon: radiation
order: 9
summary: Judging how much radiation someone received and nursing them when no hospital can help.
---

## When to use

Nausea, vomiting or diarrhoea within hours of being outside during fallout or near a radiation source.

## Steps

1. Get everyone inside and away from the fallout first.
2. Remove outer clothing, bag it, wash skin and hair with soap.
3. Write down when the exposure started and when vomiting began.
4. Vomiting within an hour means a severe dose; within 1 to 2 hours serious; 2 to 4 hours moderate; none in 6 hours is probably a low dose.
5. Rest, fluids and oral rehydration solution; paracetamol for fever and pain; small frequent meals when they can eat.
6. Keep every cut clean and covered; infection is the main killer in the following weeks while the blood counts fall.
7. Keep them away from anyone with a cough or cold and away from crowds.
8. Do not give stable iodine unless the authorities have told you to; it does nothing for radiation sickness.

## Warnings

**Warning:** Skin burns, hair loss and bleeding gums two to three weeks later mean a high dose; get medical help by any route.
**Warning:** A person who has been decontaminated is not radioactive and is safe to nurse.

## Stop or escalate

999 or 111 as soon as either works; hospital care with antibiotics and transfusions changes the odds at doses above about 2 Gy. See the [radiation module](module:radiation).

## Source

[Acute radiation syndrome (Wikipedia)](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Acute_radiation_syndrome); [FEMA nuclear detonation guidance, p. 97](doc:fema-nuclear-detonation-2022#page=97); [UKHSA decontamination sheet, p. 1](doc:ukhsa-radiation-decontamination#page=1); [Emergency War Surgery, p. 448](doc:emergency-war-surgery-2018#page=448).
```

`playbooks/cards/chemical-exposure.md`:

```markdown
---
id: chemical-exposure
title: Chemical exposure
icon: flask
order: 10
summary: Remove, remove, remove: getting a chemical off a person before it does more harm.
---

## When to use

Splashed, doused or caught in a cloud of an unknown chemical; stinging eyes, coughing, burning skin.

## Steps

1. Get away from the source, upwind and uphill, into fresh air.
2. Remove their clothing; cut it off rather than pull it over the head.
3. Remove the chemical: blot with dry tissue or cloth, do not rub.
4. If it is a powder, brush it off first; if it is caustic or in the eyes, rinse with lots of water.
5. Then wash skin and hair with plenty of water and soap if available, for 15 to 20 minutes.
6. Flush eyes with clean water for 15 minutes, eyelids held open.
7. Bag the clothing and keep it away from people. Do not induce vomiting if the chemical was swallowed.

## Warnings

**Warning:** Do not enter a cellar, tank or confined space to rescue someone; the gas that dropped them will drop you.
**Warning:** Keep the wash water away from you and from drinking water.

## Stop or escalate

999 for breathing difficulty, burns, confusion, or any exposure to an industrial release. Tell them the substance if you know it.

## Source

[Chemical emergencies, information for the public (UKHSA)](kiwix:govuk_resilience/www.gov.uk/government/publications/chemical-emergencies-information-for-the-public); [Ship Captain's Medical Guide ch. 2](doc:scmg-ch02); [HSE INDG258 Confined spaces](doc:hse-indg258).
```

`playbooks/cards/childbirth.md`:

```markdown
---
id: childbirth
title: Childbirth
icon: baby
order: 11
summary: Helping a baby arrive when no midwife can get there.
---

## When to use

Contractions every two to three minutes, the mother says the baby is coming, or the head is visible.

## Steps

1. Call 999 and stay on the line; put the phone on speaker.
2. Wash your hands. Clean towels, a warm room, privacy.
3. Let the mother choose her position; do not make her lie flat.
4. As the head appears, support it gently with a clean hand; do not pull. Let the mother push with contractions.
5. If the cord is around the neck, hook it loose with a finger. The shoulders and body come with the next pushes; hold the slippery baby firmly.
6. Dry the baby, put it skin to skin on the mother's chest, cover both, cover the baby's head. Rub the back if it does not cry.
7. Leave the cord until it stops pulsing, then tie it tightly in two places 10 and 15 cm from the baby and cut between with clean scissors. If nothing clean is available, leave it attached.
8. The placenta follows within an hour; keep it for the midwife. Massage the lower abdomen firmly and put the baby to the breast if bleeding is heavy.

## Warnings

**Warning:** Feet or bottom first, heavy bleeding before the birth, a cord coming out first, or fits are emergencies: tell 999 immediately.
**Warning:** Never pull on the cord to deliver the placenta.

## Stop or escalate

999 always. If the baby does not breathe after drying and rubbing for a minute, start [child CPR](card:cpr-child).

## Source

[Ship Captain's Medical Guide ch. 10, p. 1](doc:scmg-ch10#page=1); [Labour and birth (NHS)](kiwix:nhs_uk/www.nhs.uk/pregnancy/labour-and-birth/); [Survival and Austere Medicine, p. 282](doc:survival-austere-medicine-2017#page=282).
```

`playbooks/cards/dehydration.md`:

```markdown
---
id: dehydration
title: Dehydration
icon: water
order: 12
summary: Spotting dehydration and making oral rehydration solution from sugar and salt.
---

## When to use

Dark urine, dry mouth, dizziness, headache, tiredness; in babies a sunken soft spot, few wet nappies.

## Steps

1. Sit them in the shade or a cool room and give water in small sips.
2. Make rehydration drink: 1 litre clean water, 6 level teaspoons sugar.
3. Add half a level teaspoon of salt. Stir until clear.
4. Adults: a cup every 20 minutes, more after each loose stool.
5. Children: small sips often; keep breastfeeding or milk feeds going; aim for about a cup (200 ml) of solution after every loose stool.
6. Keep going until urine is pale and plentiful; two to three litres a day for an adult in heat or with diarrhoea.
7. Bought sachets (Dioralyte and similar) work the same way; follow the packet.
8. Avoid alcohol, strong coffee and very sugary drinks; they make it worse.

## Warnings

**Warning:** Too much salt is dangerous; measure the half teaspoon, and taste it: it should be no saltier than tears.
**Warning:** A baby under a year with diarrhoea can become dangerously dry within hours.

## Stop or escalate

999 or 111 if they are drowsy or confused, have not passed urine in 8 hours, cannot keep fluids down, have a fast weak pulse, or are a baby with a sunken soft spot or no wet nappy in 12 hours.

## Source

[Dehydration (NHS)](kiwix:nhs_uk/www.nhs.uk/conditions/dehydration/); [WHO Essential Medicines, p. 53](doc:who-eml-2025#page=53); [Oral rehydration therapy (Wikipedia)](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Oral_rehydration_therapy).
```

`playbooks/cards/wound-cleaning.md`:

```markdown
---
id: wound-cleaning
title: Wound cleaning
icon: bandage
order: 13
summary: Cleaning and dressing a wound so it does not become infected, and recognising infection and sepsis.
---

## When to use

Any cut, graze, puncture or open wound once the bleeding is under control.

## Steps

1. Wash your hands; wear gloves if you have them.
2. Rinse the wound under clean running water for at least five minutes.
3. Pick out grit and dirt with clean tweezers. Do not scrub.
4. Boiled and cooled water, or bottled water, is fine; do not use untreated stream water on a wound.
5. Pat dry with a clean cloth, apply an antiseptic such as chlorhexidine if you have it, and cover with a non-stick dressing.
6. Change the dressing daily or when wet or dirty; leave a deep or dirty wound open under a dressing rather than closing it tightly.
7. Check every day for spreading redness, heat, swelling, pus, a red line up the limb, or fever.
8. Puncture wounds, bites and anything from soil need a tetanus check if the person's five doses are not complete.

## Warnings

**Warning:** Fever, fast breathing, confusion, mottled or very pale skin, or not passing urine can be sepsis. Sepsis kills within hours.
**Warning:** Do not use hydrogen peroxide or neat bleach in a wound.

## Stop or escalate

999 for signs of sepsis. 111 or a pharmacist for spreading infection, a wound that will not close, an animal or human bite, or a wound from a dirty or rusty object.

## Source

[Cuts and grazes (NHS)](kiwix:nhs_uk/www.nhs.uk/conditions/cuts-and-grazes/); [Sepsis (NHS)](kiwix:nhs_uk/www.nhs.uk/conditions/sepsis/); [Ship Captain's Medical Guide ch. 1, p. 15](doc:scmg-ch01#page=15); [Survival and Austere Medicine, p. 231](doc:survival-austere-medicine-2017#page=231).
```

`playbooks/cards/shock.md`:

```markdown
---
id: shock
title: Shock
icon: alert
order: 14
summary: Recognising circulatory shock after bleeding, burns or severe illness, and what to do while help comes.
---

## When to use

Pale clammy skin, fast weak pulse, fast breathing, dizziness, thirst or confusion after injury or bleeding.

## Steps

1. Call 999. Treat the cause: stop bleeding, cool burns.
2. Lie them down on a blanket. Raise the legs about 30 cm.
3. Keep the legs flat if a leg, pelvis or head is injured.
4. Loosen tight clothing at the neck, chest and waist.
5. Keep them warm with blankets, but do not overheat them.
6. Nothing to eat or drink; moisten the lips if they are thirsty.
7. Reassure, stay with them, and check breathing and pulse every few minutes; if they become unresponsive but breathe, use the [recovery position](card:recovery-position).

## Warnings

**Warning:** Shock can develop slowly; a person who seems fine after a big bleed may collapse an hour later.
**Warning:** This is not emotional shock; it is failing circulation and it kills.

## Stop or escalate

999 at once. If breathing stops, start [CPR](card:cpr-adult).

## Source

[Ship Captain's Medical Guide ch. 1, p. 19](doc:scmg-ch01#page=19); [FM 4-25.11, p. 14](doc:fm-4-25-11-first-aid#page=14); [Shock (Wikipedia)](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Shock_(circulatory)).
```

`playbooks/cards/drowning.md`:

```markdown
---
id: drowning
title: Drowning
icon: waves
order: 15
summary: Getting someone out of the water safely and resuscitating them.
---

## When to use

Pulled from water and not breathing normally, or coughing, confused or exhausted after a near-drowning.

## Steps

1. Do not go in. Reach with a pole or throw something that floats.
2. Call 999; ask for the coastguard at the sea, the fire service inland.
3. Once out: check breathing for up to 10 seconds.
4. If not breathing normally: give 5 rescue breaths first, then 30 chest compressions and 2 breaths, and keep going.
5. Expect vomiting; turn them on their side to clear it, then continue.
6. If they breathe, put them in the [recovery position](card:recovery-position), strip wet clothes and cover them; treat as [hypothermia](card:hypothermia).
7. Use a defibrillator if one arrives; dry the chest first.

## Warnings

**Warning:** Anyone rescued from water must be seen by a doctor even if they seem fine; lung damage can appear hours later.
**Warning:** Cold water shock kills swimmers in minutes; do not attempt a swimming rescue.

## Stop or escalate

999 always. Continue CPR until help takes over; cold-water drowning victims have survived long resuscitations.

## Source

[Ship Captain's Medical Guide ch. 11, p. 1](doc:scmg-ch11#page=1); [Drowning (Wikipedia)](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Drowning); [CPR (NHS)](kiwix:nhs_uk/www.nhs.uk/conditions/first-aid/cpr/).
```

`playbooks/cards/seizures.md`:

```markdown
---
id: seizures
title: Seizures
icon: brain
order: 16
summary: Keeping someone safe during a fit and knowing when it is an emergency.
---

## When to use

Someone is shaking or jerking uncontrollably, has gone stiff, or is unresponsive with twitching or staring.

## Steps

1. Note the time it started. Move hard objects away.
2. Cushion the head with something soft. Do not hold them down.
3. Do not put anything in their mouth. Loosen clothing at the neck.
4. Stay with them and let the seizure run its course.
5. When the jerking stops, put them in the [recovery position](card:recovery-position) and check breathing.
6. Stay until they are fully awake; they will be confused and tired. Tell them what happened.
7. If they have a known epilepsy plan or rescue medicine, follow it.

## Warnings

**Warning:** A seizure lasting more than 5 minutes, or one after another without waking, is a medical emergency.
**Warning:** Do not give food, drink or tablets until they are fully awake.

## Stop or escalate

999 if it lasts over 5 minutes, repeats, they are injured, it happened in water, it is their first seizure, they are pregnant or diabetic, or breathing does not return to normal.

## Source

[Epilepsy (NHS)](kiwix:nhs_uk/www.nhs.uk/conditions/epilepsy/); [Epileptic seizure (Wikipedia)](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Epileptic_seizure); [Ship Captain's Medical Guide ch. 1, p. 11](doc:scmg-ch01#page=11).
```

`playbooks/cards/anaphylaxis.md`:

```markdown
---
id: anaphylaxis
title: Anaphylaxis
icon: syringe
order: 17
summary: Using an adrenaline auto-injector for a severe allergic reaction.
---

## When to use

Swollen face, lips or throat, wheeze or trouble breathing, hives or faintness after a sting, food or medicine.

## Steps

1. Use their adrenaline pen now: outer thigh, through clothing.
2. Hold it in place (Jext 10 seconds, EpiPen 3 seconds). Call 999.
3. Lie them flat with legs raised; sit them up only if breathing is hard.
4. A pregnant woman lies on her left side. Do not let them stand or walk.
5. If no better after 5 minutes, give a second pen if there is one.
6. Loosen tight clothing. Note the time of each dose for the paramedics.
7. If they become unresponsive and stop breathing, start [CPR](card:cpr-adult).

## Warnings

**Warning:** Antihistamines are not a treatment for anaphylaxis; adrenaline is.
**Warning:** Standing up suddenly during anaphylaxis can cause collapse and death.

## Stop or escalate

999, say "anaphylaxis". Hospital observation is needed even after recovery because a second reaction can follow hours later.

## Source

[Anaphylaxis (NHS)](kiwix:nhs_uk/www.nhs.uk/conditions/anaphylaxis/); [WHO Essential Medicines, p. 44](doc:who-eml-2025#page=44); [Anaphylaxis (Wikipedia)](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Anaphylaxis).
```

`playbooks/cards/carbon-monoxide.md`:

```markdown
---
id: carbon-monoxide
title: Carbon monoxide
icon: cloud
order: 18
summary: Recognising carbon monoxide poisoning from generators, stoves and barbecues and getting everyone out.
---

## When to use

Headache, dizziness, nausea or flu-like feeling in several people or pets at once, better outside.

## Steps

1. Get everyone and pets outside into fresh air now.
2. Open doors and windows on the way out if it takes no time.
3. Turn off the generator, stove, heater or barbecue if safe to do so.
4. Do not go back in. Count everyone.
5. Call the gas emergency line: 0800 111 999 in Great Britain, 0800 002 001 in Northern Ireland; 999 if anyone is unconscious or has chest pain.
6. Everyone exposed needs a medical check even if they feel better; call 111 or go to A&E.
7. Never run a generator, barbecue or camping stove indoors, in a garage, tent or conservatory; fit a CO alarm before any stove is used.

## Warnings

**Warning:** Carbon monoxide has no smell. The only warning is a CO alarm or people and animals falling ill together.
**Warning:** A charcoal barbecue keeps giving off carbon monoxide for hours after it has gone out.

## Stop or escalate

999 for anyone unconscious, confused, fitting or with chest pain; if breathing stops, [CPR](card:cpr-adult) in fresh air.

## Source

[Carbon monoxide poisoning (NHS)](kiwix:nhs_uk/www.nhs.uk/conditions/carbon-monoxide-poisoning/); [Carbon monoxide poisoning (GOV.UK)](kiwix:govuk_resilience/www.gov.uk/government/publications/carbon-monoxide-poisoning); [Approved Document J, p. 43](doc:ad-j#page=43).
```

`playbooks/cards/stroke.md`:

```markdown
---
id: stroke
title: Stroke (FAST)
icon: face
order: 19
summary: The FAST test and what to do in the first minutes of a stroke.
---

## When to use

Face drooping on one side, an arm they cannot lift, slurred speech, sudden loss of vision or balance.

## Steps

1. Face: ask them to smile. Does one side droop?
2. Arms: ask them to raise both. Does one drift down?
3. Speech: is it slurred or jumbled? Time: call 999 now.
4. Note the exact time the symptoms started; the hospital needs it.
5. Keep them still and calm, sitting or lying with the head supported; loosen tight clothing.
6. Nothing to eat or drink; swallowing is often affected.
7. If they become unresponsive but are breathing, use the [recovery position](card:recovery-position).

## Warnings

**Warning:** Symptoms that pass in minutes (a mini-stroke, TIA) still need 999; a full stroke often follows.
**Warning:** Do not give aspirin for a suspected stroke; it is deadly in the bleeding kind.

## Stop or escalate

999 at the first sign. Clot-busting treatment works only within the first hours.

## Source

[Stroke (NHS)](kiwix:nhs_uk/www.nhs.uk/conditions/stroke/); [Ship Captain's Medical Guide ch. 7, p. 34](doc:scmg-ch07#page=34); [Stroke (Wikipedia)](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Stroke).
```

`playbooks/cards/heart-attack.md`:

```markdown
---
id: heart-attack
title: Heart attack
icon: heart
order: 20
summary: Chest pain that may be a heart attack: 999, rest, aspirin.
---

## When to use

Crushing or tight chest pain, maybe spreading to arms, jaw, neck or back, with sweating or breathlessness.

## Steps

1. Call 999. Say "chest pain, possible heart attack".
2. Sit them on the floor, back against a wall, knees bent and supported.
3. Give one 300 mg aspirin to chew slowly, if not allergic.
4. If they have a GTN spray prescribed, let them use it as directed.
5. Loosen tight clothing. Keep them still and calm; no walking about.
6. Stay with them and watch their breathing.
7. If they collapse and stop breathing normally, start [CPR](card:cpr-adult) and send for a defibrillator.

## Warnings

**Warning:** Women, older people and people with diabetes often have no chest pain: just breathlessness, sweating, nausea or "indigestion".
**Warning:** Do not drive them to hospital; the ambulance carries the treatment.

## Stop or escalate

999 immediately. Every minute of delay costs heart muscle.

## Source

[Heart attack (NHS)](kiwix:nhs_uk/www.nhs.uk/conditions/heart-attack/); [Ship Captain's Medical Guide ch. 1, p. 14](doc:scmg-ch01#page=14); [Myocardial infarction (Wikipedia)](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Myocardial_infarction).
```

`playbooks/cards/recovery-position.md`:

```markdown
---
id: recovery-position
title: Recovery position
icon: bed
order: 21
summary: Placing an unconscious person who is breathing so their airway stays open.
---

## When to use

Someone is unresponsive but breathing normally, or is drowsy after a seizure, drink, drugs or a head injury.

## Steps

1. Kneel beside them. Take off glasses and empty bulky pockets.
2. Put the arm nearest you out at a right angle, elbow bent, palm up.
3. Far arm across the chest, back of that hand against the near cheek.
4. With your other hand, pull the far knee up so the foot is flat on the floor.
5. Keeping the hand against the cheek, pull the knee towards you to roll them onto their side.
6. Tilt the head back and lift the chin so the airway is open; adjust the top leg so hip and knee are at right angles.
7. Call 999 if not already done. Check breathing every minute until help arrives; turn them onto the other side after 30 minutes.

## Warnings

**Warning:** If you suspect a spinal injury, move them only if they are vomiting or you cannot keep the airway open.
**Warning:** If breathing stops, roll them onto their back and start [CPR](card:cpr-adult).

## Stop or escalate

999 for anyone who is unconscious, however briefly.

## Source

[Recovery position (NHS)](kiwix:nhs_uk/www.nhs.uk/conditions/first-aid/recovery-position/); [Ship Captain's Medical Guide ch. 1, p. 11](doc:scmg-ch01#page=11); [Recovery position (Wikipedia)](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Recovery_position).
```

`playbooks/cards/low-blood-sugar.md`:

```markdown
---
id: low-blood-sugar
title: Low blood sugar
icon: sugar
order: 22
summary: Treating a hypo in someone with diabetes, awake or unconscious.
---

## When to use

Someone with diabetes is shaky, sweaty, pale, confused or drowsy; blood glucose below 4 mmol/L if tested.

## Steps

1. If awake and able to swallow: give 15 to 20 g of fast sugar now.
2. That is 5 glucose tablets, 200 ml fruit juice, or 5 jelly babies.
3. Wait 10 to 15 minutes and give the same again if not better.
4. Once recovered, give a slower carbohydrate: a sandwich, biscuits, or their next meal.
5. Do not leave them alone until they are fully back to normal.
6. If drowsy or unconscious: nothing by mouth. Put them in the [recovery position](card:recovery-position).
7. If a glucagon injection kit is available and someone is trained, give it; call 999.

## Warnings

**Warning:** Chocolate is too slow; use sugar, glucose or fruit juice.
**Warning:** A hypo in someone on long-acting insulin or gliclazide can come back for hours; keep checking.

## Stop or escalate

999 if they are unconscious, fitting, cannot swallow, or do not improve after two rounds of sugar.

## Source

[Low blood sugar (NHS)](kiwix:nhs_uk/www.nhs.uk/conditions/low-blood-sugar-hypoglycaemia/); [Ship Captain's Medical Guide ch. 7, p. 56](doc:scmg-ch07#page=56); [Hypoglycemia (Wikipedia)](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Hypoglycemia).
```

`playbooks/cards/asthma-attack.md`:

```markdown
---
id: asthma-attack
title: Asthma attack
icon: lungs
order: 23
summary: Reliever inhaler steps for an asthma attack and when it is an emergency.
---

## When to use

Wheezing, tight chest, coughing, too breathless to finish a sentence, and the reliever inhaler is not helping.

## Steps

1. Sit them upright, leaning slightly forward. Stay calm with them.
2. One puff of the blue reliever (salbutamol) every 30 to 60 seconds.
3. Use a spacer if there is one. Up to 10 puffs.
4. If no better after 10 puffs, or they cannot speak, call 999.
5. While waiting, repeat 10 puffs after 15 minutes if the ambulance has not come.
6. Do not lie them down and do not leave them.
7. Keep windows open if the air is clean; close them if smoke, ash or pollen is the trigger.

## Warnings

**Warning:** Blue lips, exhaustion, a silent chest or drowsiness mean a life-threatening attack.
**Warning:** A brown or other preventer inhaler will not help in an attack.

## Stop or escalate

999 if 10 puffs do not help, symptoms get worse, they cannot speak, or lips go blue. After any attack, they need a GP review within 48 hours.

## Source

[Asthma attack (NHS)](kiwix:nhs_uk/www.nhs.uk/conditions/asthma/asthma-attack/); [Ship Captain's Medical Guide ch. 7, p. 7](doc:scmg-ch07#page=7); [WHO Essential Medicines, p. 64](doc:who-eml-2025#page=64).
```

```python
    "pmr446": "comms", "amateur-bands": "comms", "uk-numbers": "reference", "what-still-works": "comms",
    "household-plan": "plan", "water-disinfection": "reference", "mains-electricity": "reference",

    "ticks-adders": "reference", "about-sos": "about",
}


@pytest.mark.parametrize("slug", sorted(PAGES))
def test_page_front_matter_and_body(slug):
    post = load("pages", slug)
    assert set(post.keys()) == {"id", "title", "icon", "order", "summary", "category"}
    assert post["id"] == slug
    assert post["category"] == PAGES[slug]
    assert post["order"] == list(PAGES).index(slug) + 1
    assert len(h2(post.content)) >= 2, slug
    assert len(CITE.findall(post.content)) >= 2, slug

def test_uk_numbers_page_has_the_mandatory_numbers():

    for n in ("999", "111", "105", "0345 988 1188", "0800 111 999", "116 123", "0300 2000 100", "03457 643 643"):
        assert n in body, n


def test_pmr446_page_has_all_16_channels_and_38_tones():

    for ch in ("446.00625", "446.09375", "446.19375"):
```

- [ ] **Step 3: Write `playbooks/pages/pmr446.md` (complete exemplar)**

```markdown
---
id: pmr446
title: PMR446 channels
icon: radio
order: 1
summary: The 16 licence-free walkie-talkie channels, the CTCSS tone table and how to use them well.
category: comms
---

## The rules

PMR446 radios are licence-free across the UK and most of Europe: 0.5 W effective radiated power, a fixed antenna, 16 analogue channels 12.5 kHz apart between 446.00625 and 446.19375 MHz ([PMR446](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/PMR446); [Ofcom IR 2009](doc:ofcom-pmr446-ir2009)). Digital PMR446 (dPMR446 and DMR446) shares the same 446.0 to 446.2 MHz band. Range is one to three kilometres in a town and ten or more from a hilltop to a valley. No repeaters, no external antennas, no power boosting: a modified set is an unlicensed transmitter.

## The 16 channels

| Channel | MHz |
|---|---|
| 1 | 446.00625 |
| 2 | 446.01875 |
| 3 | 446.03125 |
| 4 | 446.04375 |
| 5 | 446.05625 |
| 6 | 446.06875 |
| 7 | 446.08125 |
| 8 | 446.09375 |
| 9 | 446.10625 |
| 10 | 446.11875 |
| 11 | 446.13125 |
| 12 | 446.14375 |
| 13 | 446.15625 |
| 14 | 446.16875 |
| 15 | 446.18125 |
| 16 | 446.19375 |

## CTCSS tones

A tone code only mutes other people's traffic on your channel; everyone on the channel still shares it. The 38 standard tones in hertz:

| Code | Hz | Code | Hz | Code | Hz | Code | Hz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 67.0 | 11 | 97.4 | 21 | 136.5 | 31 | 192.8 |
| 2 | 71.9 | 12 | 100.0 | 22 | 141.3 | 32 | 203.5 |
| 3 | 74.4 | 13 | 103.5 | 23 | 146.2 | 33 | 210.7 |
| 4 | 77.0 | 14 | 107.2 | 24 | 151.4 | 34 | 218.1 |
| 5 | 79.7 | 15 | 110.9 | 25 | 156.7 | 35 | 225.7 |
| 6 | 82.5 | 16 | 114.8 | 26 | 162.2 | 36 | 233.6 |
| 7 | 85.4 | 17 | 118.8 | 27 | 167.9 | 37 | 241.8 |
| 8 | 88.5 | 18 | 123.0 | 28 | 173.8 | 38 | 250.3 |
| 9 | 91.5 | 19 | 127.3 | 29 | 179.9 | | |
| 10 | 94.8 | 20 | 131.8 | 30 | 186.2 | | |

## Using them in an emergency

- Agree a channel and tone with your street or village and write it on the household plan ([Household plan](page:household-plan)); keep a second channel as a fallback.
- Keep messages short: who you are, where you are, what you need. Say "over" when you finish and "out" when you are done.
- Listen before you transmit; a call every 15 minutes at an agreed time saves batteries.
- Rechargeable sets die in a long outage; sets that take AA cells keep going with a torch's batteries. Keep radios warm; cold kills batteries.
- PMR446 is for local coordination. For reaching further, the amateur bands need a licence ([Amateur bands](page:amateur-bands)) and CB radio on 27 MHz is licence-free with 4 W ([Citizens band radio](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Citizens_band_radio)).

## Go deeper

- [Communications module](module:comms)
- [What still works in an outage](page:what-still-works)
- [Amateur Radio Q&A](kiwix:ham.stackexchange.com_en_all/questions)
```

```yaml
order: 2

```
Must contain: licence levels since February 2024 (Foundation 25 W, Intermediate 100 W, Full 1,000 W; callsign prefixes M7, 2E0 and M0 in England) ([Ofcom amateur licence conditions](doc:ofcom-amateur-licence-2024)); a table of the bands with edges from the RSGB plan: 160 m 1.810 to 2.000 MHz, 80 m 3.500 to 3.800, 40 m 7.000 to 7.200, 30 m 10.100 to 10.150, 20 m 14.000 to 14.350, 17 m 18.068 to 18.168, 15 m 21.000 to 21.450, 12 m 24.890 to 24.990, 10 m 28.000 to 29.700, 6 m 50.000 to 52.000, 4 m 70.000 to 70.500, 2 m 144.000 to 146.000, 70 cm 430.000 to 440.000 ([RSGB Band Plans 2026](doc:rsgb-band-plan-2026)); FM calling frequencies 145.500 MHz (2 m) and 433.500 MHz (70 cm), SSB calling 144.300 MHz; 2 m repeaters use a 600 kHz shift and 70 cm repeaters 1.6 MHz, listed in the RSGB plan; what works when: 2 m and 70 cm for local traffic, 40 m and 80 m for the UK by day and night, 20 m for Europe and beyond; a solar storm blacks out HF for hours ([Solar storm](playbook:solar-storm)); RAYNET provides communications to councils and emergency services ([RAYNET](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/RAYNET)); an unlicensed person may use a licensed station only with the licensee present and in control; in a genuine emergency a licensee may pass messages for anyone.
Headings: `## Licence levels`, `## Bands`, `## Calling and repeaters`, `## In an emergency`, `## Go deeper` (links: `page:pmr446`, `module:comms`, `kiwix:ham.stackexchange.com_en_all/questions`, `doc:rsgb-band-plan-2026`).

#### pages/uk-numbers.md

```yaml
---
id: uk-numbers
title: UK emergency numbers
icon: phone
order: 3
summary: Every number a household needs in a UK emergency, on one printable page.
category: reference
---
```
Must contain, as tables by group with a note column: **Emergency**: 999 or 112 (police, fire, ambulance, coastguard, mountain rescue via police); 999 by text after registering (text "register" to 999); Relay UK 18000; 101 police non-emergency. **Health**: 111 (England, Scotland and Wales; Northern Ireland uses GP out-of-hours numbers); NHS 111 online needs the internet; Pharmacy First in England. **Utilities**: 105 electricity power cut (Great Britain, free from any phone); NIE Networks 03457 643 643 (Northern Ireland); ESB Networks 1800 372 999 (Republic of Ireland); Isle of Man, Jersey and Guernsey: the number on your bill; gas emergency 0800 111 999 (Great Britain) and 0800 002 001 (Northern Ireland); water: your company's number from the bill, and the Priority Services Register through your supplier ([Priority Services Register](kiwix:govuk_resilience/www.thepsr.co.uk/)). **Flooding**: Floodline 0345 988 1188 (England, Scotland and Wales); Northern Ireland Flooding Incident Line 0300 2000 100; Environment Agency incident hotline 0800 80 70 60 for pollution ([Get flood warnings](kiwix:govuk_resilience/www.gov.uk/get-flood-warnings)). **Mental health**: Samaritans 116 123; Shout text 85258; CALM 0800 58 58 58; Childline 0800 1111; NHS urgent mental health via 111. **Animals**: RSPCA 0300 1234 999. **Other**: Action Fraud 0300 123 2040; Universal Credit helpline 0800 328 5644. A section on **Emergency Alerts** (no number needed; cell broadcast on 4G and 5G; how to check the phone's setting) ([How emergency alerts work](kiwix:govuk_resilience/www.gov.uk/alerts/how-alerts-work)). A section on the **network operators** by region (UK Power Networks: London, South East, East; National Grid Electricity Distribution: Midlands, South West, South Wales; SP Energy Networks: central and southern Scotland, Merseyside, Cheshire, North Wales; SSEN: north of Scotland, central southern England; Northern Powergrid: North East, Yorkshire; Electricity North West; NIE Networks; ESB Networks; Manx Utilities; Jersey Electricity; Guernsey Electricity), all reached by 105 in Great Britain ([Power module](module:power)). Blank lines to write your own: GP, pharmacy, insurer, vet, school, neighbours.
Headings: `## Emergency`, `## Health`, `## Utilities`, `## Flooding`, `## Mental health`, `## Animals and other`, `## Emergency Alerts`, `## Electricity network operators`, `## Your own numbers`.

#### pages/what-still-works.md

```yaml
---
id: what-still-works
title: What still works in an outage
icon: plug
order: 4
summary: Service by service, what keeps going when the electricity stops and for how long.
category: comms
---
```
Must contain a table with columns Service, Works?, For how long, Notes: landline on Digital Voice (no, unless the provider gave a battery unit, about one hour of calls; the copper network closes by January 2027); mobile calls and data (limited; masts mostly have about an hour of battery, some none, a few sites have generators; texts get through when calls do not; 999 uses any network); Emergency Alerts (yes while any 4G or 5G mast is up) ([How emergency alerts work](kiwix:govuk_resilience/www.gov.uk/alerts/how-alerts-work)); FM radio (yes, on batteries or wind-up; BBC Radio 4 on 92 to 95 MHz and BBC local radio); DAB (yes but drains batteries faster); Radio 4 long wave 198 kHz (closing in 2026); TV (no); home internet (no, the router and the fibre box need power); gas boiler (no, needs electricity); gas hob (yes, light with a match); mains water (usually for hours to days; not in high-rise flats) ([CMO outage advice](kiwix:govuk_resilience/www.gov.uk/government/publications/public-health-advice-from-uk-cmos-during-a-national-power-outage)); sewers (usually, unless a pumping station fails); fridge (about 4 hours shut) and freezer (24 to 48 hours) ([FSA](kiwix:govuk_resilience/www.gov.uk/government/publications/how-to-chill-freeze-and-defrost-food-safely)); cash machines and card payments (no); petrol pumps (no); traffic lights (no); trains (electric no, diesel limited); lifts and electric gates (no; use the manual release); grid-tied solar panels (no, unless a battery with an islanding switch) ([Solar islanding](page:solar-islanding)); an electric car with vehicle-to-load (yes, for days); hospitals (generators); this box on a power bank (12 to 15 hours). Then `## What to do about it` (battery radio, power bank, cash, paper numbers, a battery unit for Digital Voice if anyone in the house relies on the landline, Priority Services Register) and `## Go deeper` (`page:uk-numbers`, `module:power`, `module:comms`, `playbook:grid-collapse`, `kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Public_switched_telephone_network`).

#### pages/household-plan.md

```yaml
---
id: household-plan
title: Household emergency plan
icon: clipboard
order: 5
summary: A fill-in plan for your household, following the government's template, to print and keep on the fridge.
category: plan
---
```
Must contain: an introduction saying it follows the government's Household Emergency Plan ([Prepare](kiwix:prepare_uk/prepare.campaign.gov.uk/get-prepared-for-emergencies/)) and that the box's notes on `/plan` hold the filled-in copy; then fill-in sections as headings each with a short prompt and blank table rows: `## Who lives here` (name, needs, medicines, mobile); `## Where we meet` (near the house, outside the area); `## Our out-of-area contact`; `## Numbers` (link to [UK numbers](page:uk-numbers)); `## Where things are` (stopcock, gas valve, consumer unit, torch, radio, first-aid kit, grab bags, this box); `## Insurance and documents` (policy numbers, where the passports are); `## Pets and animals`; `## Water and food` (litres stored, date to rotate: 2.5 to 3 litres per person per day for at least three days per Prepare); `## Neighbours who may need help`; `## How we get warnings` (Emergency Alerts on, radio frequency, flood warnings sign-up ([Get flood warnings](kiwix:govuk_resilience/www.gov.uk/get-flood-warnings))); `## Who does what`; `## Review date`. Also a `## Community plan` note pointing to [Community](module:community) and Ready Scotland's templates ([Ready Scotland](kiwix:govuk_resilience/ready.campaign.gov.scot/community-emergency-planning)).

#### pages/water-disinfection.md

```yaml
---
id: water-disinfection
title: Water disinfection
icon: water
order: 6
summary: Boiling, UK bleach, NaDCC tablets, iodine, sunlight and filters, with doses and waiting times in one table.
category: reference
---
```
Must contain: `## The table` with columns Method, Dose per litre, Wait, Kills, Does not remove: boil (rolling boil 1 minute; bacteria, viruses, parasites; chemicals, radioactive particles); thin unscented bleach 4.5 to 5% (2 drops, 0.1 ml; 30 minutes; double for cloudy); NaDCC tablets (8.5 mg per litre, 17 mg per 2 litres, 67 mg per 20 litres; 30 minutes; double for cloudy) ([Sodium dichloroisocyanurate](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Sodium_dichloroisocyanurate)); 2% tincture of iodine (5 drops clear, 10 cloudy; 30 minutes; not for pregnant women, thyroid patients or more than a few weeks); SODIS (clear PET bottle, 6 hours full sun, 2 days cloudy) ([Solar water disinfection](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Solar_water_disinfection)); hollow-fibre or ceramic filters 0.1 to 0.2 micron (bacteria, parasites; not viruses or chemicals). `## UK bleach` explaining thick versus thin bleach, reading the label for sodium hypochlorite percentage and "no perfume, no thickener", and never using it if in doubt. `## Cloudy, coloured or smelly water`: settle, pour off, filter through cloth, then treat; never treat water with fuel, chemical or sewage contamination for drinking. `## Storing water`: clean sealed containers, dark and cool, rotate every six months; sanitise containers with a teaspoon of thin bleach in a litre of water, swirl, rinse ([Water Treatment Library](kiwix:zimgit-water_en/home)). `## Go deeper` (`module:water`, `card:dehydration`, `kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Water_purification`, `kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Boil-water_advisory`).

#### pages/mains-electricity.md

```yaml
---
id: mains-electricity
title: Mains electricity
icon: bolt
order: 7
summary: How a UK home is wired (230 volts, ring mains, RCDs, plug fuses) and the rules for touching any of it.
category: reference
---
```
Must contain: 230 V single phase at 50 Hz; the consumer unit with a main switch, 30 mA RCDs and miniature circuit breakers (typically 6 A lighting, 16 or 20 A radials, 32 A ring finals in 2.5 mm² cable, 32 or 40 A cooker and shower circuits); BS 1363 plugs with 3 A or 13 A fuses; earthing types (TN-S, TN-C-S, TT) and why a generator needs its own earth; the RCD test button every three months ([HSE INDG231, p. 3](doc:hse-indg231#page=3)); Part P: new circuits, consumer units and work in bathrooms are notifiable and need a registered electrician ([Approved Document P, p. 7](doc:ad-p#page=7)); isolate before touching, prove dead, never work live; extension leads unwound, not daisy-chained; water and electricity; generators only through a transfer switch ([Power](module:power)); background ([Electrical wiring in the United Kingdom](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Electrical_wiring_in_the_United_Kingdom), [Ring circuit](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Ring_circuit), [Residual-current device](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Residual-current_device)). Headings: `## The numbers`, `## The consumer unit`, `## What you may and may not do`, `## Generators and batteries`, `## Go deeper`.

#### pages/solar-islanding.md

```yaml
---
id: solar-islanding
title: Solar panels in a power cut
icon: sun
order: 8
summary: Why a normal solar system switches off with the grid, and what it takes to keep the lights on.
category: reference
---
```
Must contain: grid-tied inverters must disconnect when the grid fails (loss-of-mains protection under the G98 and G99 connection rules) so linesmen are not electrocuted, so panels on the roof produce nothing usable in an outage ([Islanding](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Islanding), [Solar inverter](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Solar_inverter)); the fixes: a hybrid inverter with a battery and an emergency power supply (EPS) output, a manual changeover switch feeding a separate circuit, both installed and notified under Part P ([Approved Document P, p. 9](doc:ad-p#page=9)); or a portable "solar generator" (a 1 kWh lithium power station and a folding 100 to 200 W panel) that runs a fridge for about a day and charges phones and radios for weeks; UK yield reality: a 1 kWp array gives roughly 0.5 to 1 kWh a day in December and 4 to 5 in June, so a 100 W panel in winter is a phone charger, not a heater; a car's 12 V socket through a 150 W inverter as the fallback ([Vehicles and fuel](module:vehicles-fuel)); LiFePO4 batteries hold 1,280 Wh per 100 Ah at 12.8 V and tolerate thousands of cycles ([Lithium iron phosphate battery](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Lithium_iron_phosphate_battery)); further reading in the energy wiki ([energypedia](kiwix:energypedia_en_all_maxi/Main_Page)). Headings: `## Why the panels go dark`, `## Keeping some power`, `## What a panel gives in Britain`, `## Go deeper`.

#### pages/knife-firearms-law.md

```yaml
---
id: knife-firearms-law
title: Knife and firearms law
icon: shield
order: 9
summary: Plain-English answers on knives, air guns, crossbows, sprays and self-defence in England, Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland.
category: reference
---
```
Must contain, as questions with short answers: "Can I carry a penknife?" (a folding, non-locking blade of 3 inches, 7.62 cm, or less, yes; anything locking or longer needs a good reason such as work or a planned activity, and "self-defence" is not one) ([CJA 1988 s139](kiwix:legislation_uk/www.legislation.gov.uk/ukpga/1988/33/section/139), [GOV.UK knives](kiwix:govuk_resilience/www.gov.uk/buying-carrying-knives)); "What can I not own even at home?" (the prohibited list under s141 and the Offensive Weapons Act 2019, zombie-style knives and machetes since September 2024, ninja swords since August 2025, knuckledusters, batons, disguised knives) ([Offensive Weapons Act 2019](kiwix:legislation_uk/www.legislation.gov.uk/ukpga/2019/17/contents)); "Pepper spray or a stun gun?" (prohibited weapons, five years' imprisonment possible) ([Firearms Act 1968 s5](kiwix:legislation_uk/www.legislation.gov.uk/ukpga/1968/27/section/5)); "Air rifles?" (England and Wales: no licence under 12 ft·lb rifle or 6 ft·lb pistol, 18 to buy; Scotland: an air weapon certificate ([Air Weapons and Licensing (Scotland) Act 2015](kiwix:legislation_uk/www.legislation.gov.uk/asp/2015/10/contents)); Northern Ireland: a firearm certificate above 1 joule); "Shotguns and rifles?" (shotgun and firearm certificates from the police) ([firearms licensing](kiwix:govuk_resilience/www.gov.uk/guidance/firearms-licensing-police-guidance)); "Crossbows, catapults, bows?" (legal to own from 18, no licence; using any of them on wild animals is prohibited under the Wildlife and Countryside Act 1981 s11 ([WCA 1981 s1](kiwix:legislation_uk/www.legislation.gov.uk/ukpga/1981/69/section/1))); "Can I defend my home?" (reasonable force; for a householder against an intruder, unlawful only if grossly disproportionate ([CJIA 2008 s76](kiwix:legislation_uk/www.legislation.gov.uk/ukpga/2008/4/section/76)); no traps, no weapons kept for the purpose); "Scotland and Northern Ireland?" (the s49 equivalent ([Criminal Law (Consolidation) (Scotland) Act 1995 s49](kiwix:legislation_uk/www.legislation.gov.uk/ukpga/1995/39/section/49)), Northern Ireland's stricter firearms and air weapon rules through the Police Service of Northern Ireland). Headings: `## Knives`, `## Prohibited weapons`, `## Air weapons, shotguns and rifles`, `## Crossbows, catapults and bows`, `## Defending yourself and your home`, `## Scotland and Northern Ireland`, `## Go deeper` (`module:security-law`, `page:foraging-law`).

#### pages/foraging-law.md

```yaml
---
id: foraging-law
title: Foraging, fishing and hunting law
icon: leaf
order: 10
summary: What you may pick, catch and shoot in the UK, where, and in which season.
category: reference
---
```
Must contain: picking wild fruit, foliage, fungi and flowers for your own use is not theft (Theft Act 1968 s4(3)), selling it is; uprooting any wild plant without the landowner's permission is an offence and Schedule 8 plants may not be picked at all (Wildlife and Countryside Act 1981 s13) ([Theft Act s4](kiwix:legislation_uk/www.legislation.gov.uk/ukpga/1968/60/section/4), [WCA s13](kiwix:legislation_uk/www.legislation.gov.uk/ukpga/1981/69/section/13)); byelaws on many nature reserves, the New Forest and Epping Forest forbid picking fungi; the foreshore is mostly Crown Estate and seaweed and shellfish need permission; shellfish from unknown water carry norovirus, E. coli and shellfish poisoning; the FSA's foraging rules on contamination (roadsides, dog areas, sprayed fields), correct identification and cooking ([FSA safe foraging](kiwix:govuk_resilience/www.gov.uk/government/publications/guidance-for-safe-foraging/guidance-for-safe-foraging)); fishing: a rod licence from the Environment Agency in England and Wales for anyone 13 or over, plus the owner's permission; coarse-fish close season on rivers 15 March to 15 June ([rod fishing rules](kiwix:govuk_resilience/www.gov.uk/freshwater-rod-fishing-rules), [buy a licence](kiwix:govuk_resilience/www.gov.uk/fishing-licences/buy-a-fishing-licence)); no licence for sea fishing from the shore; Scotland needs no rod licence but permission; Northern Ireland needs a DAERA licence and a permit; hunting: no dogs for hunting mammals (Hunting Act 2004), deer only in season with a certificated rifle of the legal calibre (Deer Act 1991), game seasons (pheasant 1 October to 1 February, grouse 12 August to 10 December), rabbits and pigeons with the landowner's permission ([Hunting Act 2004](kiwix:legislation_uk/www.legislation.gov.uk/ukpga/2004/37/contents), [Deer Act 1991](kiwix:legislation_uk/www.legislation.gov.uk/ukpga/1991/54/contents), [GOV.UK hunting](kiwix:govuk_resilience/www.gov.uk/hunting)); snares are banned in Wales (2023) and Scotland (2024); eggs and nesting birds are protected everywhere. Headings: `## Plants and fungi`, `## The coast`, `## Fishing`, `## Hunting and shooting`, `## Go deeper` (`module:food`, `module:security-law`, `page:knife-firearms-law`, `kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Oenanthe_crocata`, `kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Amanita_phalloides`).

#### pages/ticks-adders.md

```yaml
---
id: ticks-adders
title: Ticks and adders
icon: bug
order: 11
summary: Removing ticks, spotting Lyme disease, and what to do about Britain's one venomous snake.
category: reference
---
```
Must contain: ticks: grasp with a tick tool or fine tweezers as close to the skin as possible and pull straight out, never squeeze, burn or smother; clean the bite; a spreading circular rash (erythema migrans) three days to a month later, or flu-like illness, means Lyme disease and needs a GP or 111 for antibiotics (doxycycline is prescribed, usually for three weeks) ([Lyme disease (NHS)](kiwix:nhs_uk/www.nhs.uk/conditions/lyme-disease/), [Lyme disease](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Lyme_disease)); prevention: long trousers tucked in, check the whole body daily, ticks favour bracken and long grass from spring to autumn; adders: the only venomous snake in Britain (none in Ireland), about 100 bites a year, deaths extremely rare; keep the person still, immobilise the bitten limb below heart level, remove rings and watches, note the time; no tourniquet, no cutting, no sucking, no ice; 999 or A&E, where antivenom is held; dogs are bitten more often than people ([Snake bites (NHS)](kiwix:nhs_uk/www.nhs.uk/conditions/snake-bites/), [Vipera berus](kiwix:wikipedia_en_all_maxi/Vipera_berus)). Headings: `## Ticks`, `## Lyme disease`, `## Adders`, `## Go deeper` (`module:medical`, `card:anaphylaxis`, `card:wound-cleaning`).

#### pages/about-sos.md

```yaml
---
id: about-sos
title: About Operation SOS
icon: info
order: 12
summary: What this box is, where its content comes from, the licences, and what it is not.
category: about
---
```
Must contain: `## What it is` (an offline, UK-focused knowledge box: playbooks, the NHS website snapshot, Wikipedia, manuals, maps; works on its own screen and over its WiFi with no internet); `## Where the content comes from` (every library item shows an "as at" date and a licence; NHS pages are a dated snapshot under the Open Government Licence v3 and carry the line "Information from the NHS website ... as at" the crawl date; map data is OpenStreetMap under ODbL and Ordnance Survey under OGL; the playbooks are written from the cited sources, each citation opens the source) ([National Risk Register 2025](doc:nrr-2025), [Prepare](kiwix:prepare_uk/prepare.campaign.gov.uk/about-this-website-and-uk-resilience/)); `## What it is not` (not a medical device and not medical advice: it shows official pages and first-aid steps and tells you when to call 999 or 111; the assistant, when switched on, only quotes from the library and can be wrong; the library pages are the source of truth; not affiliated with the NHS or the government; no tracking, no accounts, nothing leaves the box); `## Keeping it current` (the owner runs an update over ethernet; the system screen shows when each item was refreshed; Emergency Alerts, weather warnings and flood warnings are live services this box cannot replace) ([Emergency Alerts](kiwix:govuk_resilience/www.gov.uk/alerts)); `## Credits` (Kiwix and openZIM, Wikipedia and its contributors, OpenStreetMap contributors, Ordnance Survey OpenData, Protomaps, MapLibre, llama.cpp, the authors of the public-domain manuals). The page never names any other product.

- [ ] **Step 5: Run the tests and the validator**

Run: `cd api && pytest tests/test_playbooks_content.py -q`
Expected: `111 passed`.

Run: `sos validate-playbooks; echo "exit $?"`
Expected: `exit 1`; the only remaining errors are the 20 missing scenarios (and the `playbook:` links to them from pages and modules).

- [ ] **Step 6: Commit**

```bash
git add playbooks/pages api/tests/test_playbooks_content.py
git commit -m "feat(content): add the 12 comms, reference, plan and about pages"
```

**Fact-check checklist (reviewer, Task 5):**
- [ ] pmr446: all 16 frequencies and 38 tones against the Wikipedia PMR446 and CTCSS tables in the library.
- [ ] amateur-bands: band edges against the RSGB 2026 plan PDF; power limits against the Ofcom licence booklet.
- [ ] uk-numbers: every number dialled once from a UK mobile (or checked against the organisation's own site) on the review date; NI and RoI numbers included.
- [ ] what-still-works: Digital Voice battery hour and the PSTN closure date against the provider's page; mast battery statement worded as "about an hour for most masts".
- [ ] household-plan: matches the sections of the current HMG Household Emergency Plan document from prepare.campaign.gov.uk.
- [ ] water-disinfection: bleach percentage on three UK supermarket thin bleaches read from the label; NaDCC pack doses.
- [ ] mains-electricity: MCB ratings typical; Part P notifiable list against AD P page 7.
- [ ] solar-islanding: G98/G99 names current; yield figures within the range on a current MCS or Energy Saving Trust estimate.
- [ ] knife-firearms-law: ban dates (24 September 2024 zombie-style knives, 1 August 2025 ninja swords) and NI 1-joule air weapon rule.
- [ ] foraging-law: rod licence age (13) and close season dates against the GOV.UK pages; snare ban years for Wales and Scotland.
- [ ] ticks-adders: NHS Lyme page content; adder statistics.
- [ ] about-sos: the NHS attribution wording matches the NHS website terms clause 3.6.
